    "oldString": "    \\item We quantify task-specific convergence: only retrieval tasks (LAMBADA) show attention-driven cross-architecture convergence, and this pattern is scale-dependent (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}; \\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}, Table~\\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}).\n\\end{itemize}",
    "newString": "    \\item We quantify task-specific convergence: only retrieval tasks (LAMBADA) show attention-driven cross-architecture convergence, and this pattern is scale-dependent (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}; \\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}, Table~\\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}).\n    \\item We demonstrate that aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled: cross-architecture $\\sigma$ decreases monotonically with attention ratio while pairwise Jaccard saturates at the first attention level, formally operationalized via confidence-interval overlap conditions (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling}).\n\\end{itemize}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nWhen attention layers are interleaved into recurrent architectures, they help and hurt almost entirely different examples---pairwise Jaccard overlap among three hybrid designs is at most 0.10, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: on LAMBADA, cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79\\% as attention increases (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; the BCa 95\\% CI excludes zero), but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by rescuing and disrupting different inputs. Practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores; architectures that score identically in aggregate may thus carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nMost evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate benchmark scores and report that a given attention ratio achieves a certain accuracy averaged across tasks or averaged across architectures. This practice can mask substantial per-example behavioral diversity. \\citet{marx2020predictive} formalized this risk as predictive multiplicity: a prediction problem admits competing models with conflicting predictions on individual inputs. \\citet{damour2022underspecification} documented a related phenomenon, underspecification, showing that models trained from the same pipeline with different random seeds can disagree on individual predictions despite equivalent aggregate performance. \\citet{mccoy2020berts} demonstrated a concrete NLP instance: BERT models trained from different random seeds diverge on specific linguistic constructions. These studies examine variation from initialization or training noise within a single architecture. Whether predictive multiplicity extends to hybrid recurrent architectures, where the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, remains unquantified. The question matters because practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmarks; if architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, such choices carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\\% to 25\\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On four benchmarks---LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$), HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$), PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$), and ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$)---we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat.}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields three findings. First, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent variation. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval). Second, attention-driven aggregate convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$, heuristic given $n = 3$, \\S\\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}; cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%). LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is present at 1.3B but absent at 340M (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}). On LAMBADA, aggregate $\\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \\to 0.005$) while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4\\% attention (${\\sim}0.09$)---the two quantities are decoupled. The experiments that follow begin with convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}) because it establishes the aggregate backdrop against which per-example divergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}) and decoupling (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) are measured.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item We show that architectural variation---under matched training conditions---produces structured per-example predictive multiplicity with a task-dependent variation ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks in three recurrent backbones, with magnitude independent of aggregate convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). Unlike seed-driven disagreement within a single architecture \\citep{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, this multiplicity is not addressable by seed averaging: the source of variation is the recurrent backbone rather than random initialization, though we cannot fully disentangle the two with single training runs (Limitations).\n    \\item We quantify task-specific convergence: only retrieval tasks (LAMBADA) show attention-driven cross-architecture convergence, and this pattern is scale-dependent (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}; \\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}, Table~\\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

Unlike seed-driven disagreement within a single architecture \\citep{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, this multiplicity is not addressable by seed averaging: the source of variation is the recurrent backbone rather than random initialization, though we cannot fully disentangle the two with single training runs (Limitations).",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of attention effects in hybrid recurrent models. Per-example multiplicity spans a task-dependent spectrum ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks, with Jaccard uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$) regardless of aggregate convergence, suggesting diverse hybrid ensembles may offer complementary coverage. Aggregate convergence, by contrast, is task-specific (only LAMBADA) and present at 1.3B but absent at 340M.\n\nFor practitioners selecting among hybrid architectures, these results suggest a concrete protocol. Per-example audits are warranted whenever multiple hybrid designs achieve comparable aggregate scores on a target task: the low Jaccard ($\\leq 0.10$, indicating ${\\sim}90\\%$ non-overlap) means that architectures which appear interchangeable by aggregate metrics affect nearly disjoint sets of examples. In deployment contexts where prediction consistency matters---medical, legal, or safety-critical applications---aggregate equivalence does not imply per-example equivalence, and architecture choice may determine which specific inputs are handled correctly. Low cross-architecture Jaccard also suggests potential complementarity for ensembles, though realizing such gains requires empirical validation beyond this observational study.\n",

  "new_string": "A proof-of-concept majority vote across three architectures does not outperform the best individual architecture (LAMBADA: $-0.2$\\,pp; ARC-Easy: $-1.1$\\,pp), despite 15--45\\% per-example disagreement. Oracle accuracy---correct if any architecture is correct---reveals substantial complementarity potential ($+7$--$14$\\,pp above best), but majority vote captures only 1--9\\% of this ceiling. This gap quantifies the structural cost of multiplicity: when two of three architectures agree on the wrong answer, the correct minority prediction is inaccessible to naive ensembling, motivating future work on architecture-aware selection methods."
}

Per-example multiplicity spans a task-dependent variation ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks, with Jaccard uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$) regardless of aggregate convergence. Aggregate convergence, by contrast, is task-specific (only LAMBADA) and present at 1.3B but absent at 340M.\n\nFor practitioners selecting among hybrid architectures, these results suggest a concrete protocol. Per-example audits are warranted whenever multiple hybrid designs achieve comparable aggregate scores on a target task: the low Jaccard ($\\leq 0.10$, indicating ${\\sim}90\\%$ non-overlap) means that architectures which appear interchangeable by aggregate metrics affect nearly disjoint sets of examples. In deployment contexts where prediction consistency matters---medical, legal, or safety-critical applications---aggregate equivalence does not imply per-example equivalence, and architecture choice may determine which specific inputs are handled correctly. Low cross-architecture Jaccard also suggests potential complementarity for ensembles, though realizing such gains requires empirical validation beyond this observational study.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 2,
        "oldLines": 4,
        "newStart": 2,
        "newLines": 4,

          " ",
          " We present the first systematic per-example analysis of attention effects in hybrid recurrent models. Per-example multiplicity spans a task-dependent variation ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks, with Jaccard uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$) regardless of aggregate convergence. Aggregate convergence, by contrast, is task-specific (only LAMBADA) and present at 1.3B but absent at 340M.",
          " ",
          "-For practitioners selecting among hybrid architectures, these results suggest a concrete protocol. Per-example audits are warranted whenever multiple hybrid designs achieve comparable aggregate scores on a target task: the low Jaccard ($\\leq 0.10$, indicating ${\\sim}90\\%$ non-overlap) means that architectures which appear interchangeable by aggregate metrics affect nearly disjoint sets of examples. In deployment contexts where prediction consistency matters---medical, legal, or safety-critical applications---aggregate equivalence does not imply per-example equivalence, and architecture choice may determine which specific inputs are handled correctly. Low cross-architecture Jaccard also suggests potential complementarity for ensembles, though realizing such gains requires empirical validation beyond this observational study.",
          "+For practitioners selecting among hybrid architectures, these results suggest a concrete protocol. Per-example audits are warranted whenever multiple hybrid designs achieve comparable aggregate scores on a target task: the low Jaccard ($\\leq 0.10$, indicating ${\\sim}90\\%$ non-overlap) means that architectures which appear interchangeable by aggregate metrics affect nearly disjoint sets of examples. In deployment contexts where prediction consistency matters---medical, legal, or safety-critical applications---aggregate equivalence does not imply per-example equivalence, and architecture choice may determine which specific inputs are handled correctly. A proof-of-concept majority vote across three architectures does not outperform the best individual architecture (LAMBADA: $-0.2$\\,pp; ARC-Easy: $-1.1$\\,pp), despite 15--45\\% per-example disagreement. Oracle accuracy---correct if any architecture is correct---reveals substantial complementarity potential ($+7$--$14$\\,pp above best), but majority vote captures only 1--9\\% of this ceiling. This gap quantifies the structural cost of multiplicity: when two of three architectures agree on the wrong answer, the correct minority prediction is inaccessible to naive ensembling, motivating future work on architecture-aware selection methods."
